Rocky Mountain Power Transmission Line Upgrade CUP Staff Report – September 10, 2024 
 
 
 
STAFF REPORT 
 
To:   Snyderville Basin Planning Commission   
From:   Amir Caus, County Planner 
Date of Meeting: September 10, 2024 
Type of Item:  Conditional Use Permit - Public Hearing, Possible Action 
Process:  Administrative Review 
File #:   24-053 
 
Recommendation:  Staff has reviewed the application for compliance with all standards in 
the Snyderville Basin Development Code and has found that it meets the minimum required for 
approval. Staff recommends that the Snyderville Basin Planning Commission review the 
proposed Rocky Mountain Power Transmission Line Upgrade Quinn's Jct. to Tuhaye Conditional 
Use Permit, conduct a public hearing and approve the application pursuant to the findings of 
fact, conclusions of law and conditions of approval found in this Staff Report.   
 
Project Description 
 
Project Name: Rocky Mountain Power Transmission Line Upgrade Quinn's Jct. to 
Tuhaye Conditional Use Permit 
Applicant(s):   Andrew Badger, Rocky Mountain Power 
Property Owner(s):  Rocky Mountain Power via ownership and easements shown in 
Location:               North of the S.R. 248 corridor area going eastbound from Quinn’s  
    Junction to Kamas (~2,000 feet) – see maps in Exhibit package 
Zone District:   Service Commercial (SC) Zone, Hillside Stewardship (HS) Zone, and 
    Lower Silver Creek Soils Overlay Zone 
Parcel Number and Size: S-1067 (15.45 acres), and SS-64-1000-UP-X (area of work ~0.68  
    acres)  
Type of Process:  Administrative 
Final Land Use Authority: Snyderville Basin Planning Commission 
 
Proposal 
 
The Summit County Community Development Department has received an application for a 
Conditional Use Permit for a Rocky Mountain Power transmission line upgrade between 
Quinn’s Junction (Rocky Mountain Power Substation) and Tuhaye. The applicant is requesting 
to upgrade ~6.2 miles of an existing Rocky Mountain Power transmission line. The purpose of 
the project is to upgrade the lines as part of an ongoing effort for wildfire mitigation which 
includes fire wrapping, pole replacement, line replacement, and average height decrease in 
cases where poles are being replaced. According to the applicant, the lines will be rebuilt to the 
6
2 
Rocky Mountain Power Transmission Line Upgrade CUP Staff Report – September 10, 2024 
up-to-date national electric safety codes and avian safe standards. The upgrade will 
accommodate existing and future electric power needs for Summit County.  
 
The upgrades taking place within unincorporated Summit County are taking place over a 
~2,000-foot span and will continue into Park City and Tuhaye. Each applicable jurisdiction will 
review this under their own applicable land use requirements within their respective 
boundaries.  
 
Vicinity Map 
 
 
 
Background 
 
According to the applicant, the existing transmission line alignment has been in existence for 
over 40 years and needs to be upgraded. 
 
Per the Snyderville Basin Development Code, “Utility Facilities, Aboveground” are defined as 
“Utility towers and transmission and distribution towers greater than forty five feet (45') in 
height” and are reviewed under a Low Impact Permit in the SC zone district and under a 
Conditional Use Permit in the HS zone district.  
 
Section 10-3-2 (C) of the Code states: “The director or county council may, in their discretion, 
combine the processing and hearing of development permits, procedures for legislative actions, 
or requests for major or minor development review, conditional use permit, or low impact 
permit.”  
7
3 
Rocky Mountain Power Transmission Line Upgrade CUP Staff Report – September 10, 2024 
 
This application is being processed as a Conditional Use Permit, per a decision from the 
Community Development Director.  
 
Analysis and Findings 
 
Section 10-3-5(B) of the Snyderville Basin Development Code states that no Conditional Use 
Permit shall be approved unless the applicant demonstrates that the following standards can be 
met:  
 
Standard 1: The use is in accordance with the General Plan.  
  
Analysis: Policy 7.2 of the Snyderville Basin General Plan states: “Essential facilities 
should be designed to provide an acceptable level of service to the peak service demand. 
Other facilities should be designed to accommodate average demand. Temporarily 
reduced service levels may occur at times of peak service demand; however, reductions 
should not produce a meaningful threat to the public safety.” 
 
Electric infrastructure is essential to accommodate the needs of both the current and 
potential future population of Summit County. This application seeks to improve existing 
infrastructure to meet safety standards and increased demand. COMPLIES 
 
Standard 2: The use conforms to all applicable provisions of this Title, including, but not limited 
to, any applicable provisions of this Section and Chapter 4 of the Snyderville Basin Development 
Code, the Snyderville Basin General Plan, and State and Federal regulations.  
 
Analysis: Staff review of the application indicates that it meets the minimum 
requirements for approval in the Snyderville Basin Development Code.

Standard 3: The use is not detrimental to public health, safety and welfare.  
 
Analysis: The proposed improvements within the unincorporated Summit County take 
place within the Lower Silver Creek Soils Temporary Overlay Zone. Per Summit County 
9
5 
Rocky Mountain Power Transmission Line Upgrade CUP Staff Report – September 10, 2024 
Code Title 4 Health and Sanitation; “Anyone desiring to develop or redevelop in the 
overlay zone shall obtain a soils study and shall show evidence that the development 
area is outside of the impacted area or shall propose a plan to remediate any 
environmental problems/violations identified in the study to the satisfaction of UDEQ 
and EPA before Summit County will grant a development permit.” 
 
The applicant has provided a soils study and has received Utah Department of 
Environmental Quality (UDEQ)/Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) approval for the 
proposed pole replacement taking place within the Lower Silver Creek Soils Temporary 
Overlay Zone.  
 
Due to the minimal disturbance area and updated safety standards, Staff does not 
anticipate any negative impacts. COMPLIES 
 
Standard 4: The use is appropriately located with respect to public facilities.  
   
Analysis: The use is an existing utility use and will continue as such. Should the need 
arise to increase capacity and/or intensity of the use, an amendment to the Conditional 
Use would be required. COMPLIES 
 
Standard 5: The use is compatible with the existing neighborhood character and with the 
character and purpose provision of the applicable zoning district, and will not adversely affect 
surrounding land uses.   
 
Analysis: The applicant is updating an existing transmission line. The proposed use is in 
the vicinity of other utility facilities. The use is compatible with the existing 
neighborhood. COMPLIES  
 
Section 10-3-5(C) Special Standards For Conditional Uses states; 
 
In addition to the standards established in this section and in chapter 4 of this title for particular 
uses, all conditional uses within a zoning district shall conform to the following standards and 
criteria:  
 
1. The commission may require the applicant or the owner of the property subject to an 
application for development approval for a conditional use permit to establish an escrow 
account, post a bond or provide other financial security, in such form and sum as the 
commission shall determine, with sufficient surety running to the county to offset any 
extraordinary costs or expenses associated with the following: a) construction of any 
highways, roads, water or sewer mains, drainage facilities, or other public infrastructure; 
b) landscaping; c) compliance with the requirements of this section, any applicable 
special requirements set forth in this section and chapter 4 of this title, and the 
conditions attached to the development permit; and d) any expense requirements set 
forth in this section and chapter 4 of this title, and the conditions attached to the

development permit, including the provision of facilities or structures, maintenance or 
construction work, or the execution or fulfillment of conditions of a continuing nature. 
 
Analysis: Before issuance of construction permits, Summit County Engineering 
Department requires Development Improvement Agreements. COMPLIES 
 
2. The proposed development shall not cause a reduction in the adopted level of service for 
any public facility. 
 
Analysis: The applicant is proposing to update an existing transmission line in order to 
increase the level of service for electrical utility needs. COMPLIES

 of this title with respect to exterior lighting.

preserved and used, where possible, through careful location and design of circulation 
ways, buildings and other structures, parking areas, recreation areas, open space, 
utilities and drainage facilities. 
 
Analysis: The applicant proposes to mostly use existing approaches and access points. 
Any new disturbance would be restored. COMPLIES 
 
5. All roads shall provide free movement for safe and efficient use within the development. 
Local roads shall provide access to the site in a manner that discourages unsafe and

Recommendation 
 
It is Staff’s finding that the project meets the applicable standards in the Snyderville Basin 
Development Code. Staff recommends that the Snyderville Basin Planning Commission review 
and approve the Rocky Mountain Power Transmission Line Upgrade Quinn's Jct. to Tuhaye 
Conditional Use Permit according to the following Findings of Fact, Conclusions of Law and 
Conditions of Approval:  
 
Findings of Fact: 
 
1. The applicant is requesting to upgrade ~6.2 miles of an existing Rocky Mountain Power 
transmission line as per Exhibit A of the Staff Report. 
2. The purpose of the project is to upgrade the lines as part of an ongoing effort for 
wildfire mitigation.  
3. According to the applicant, the lines will be rebuilt to the up-to-date national electric 
safety codes and avian safe standards.  
4. The upgrades taking place within unincorporated Summit County are taking place over a 
~2,000-foot span and will continue into Park City and Tuhaye.  
5. Each applicable jurisdiction will review this under their own applicable land use 
requirements within their respective boundaries.  
6. According to the applicant, the existing transmission line alignment has been in 
existence for over 40 years and needs to be upgraded. 
12
8 
Rocky Mountain Power Transmission Line Upgrade CUP Staff Report – September 10, 2024 
7. According to the applicant, the upgrade will accommodate existing and future electric 
power needs for Summit County. 
8. Per the Snyderville Basin Development Code, “Utility Facilities, Aboveground” are 
defined as “Utility towers and transmission and distribution towers greater than forty 
five feet (45') in height” and are reviewed under a Low Impact Permit in the SC zone 
district and under a Conditional Use Permit in the HS zone district.  
9. Section 10-3-2 (C) of the Code states: “The director or county council may, in their 
discretion, combine the processing and hearing of development permits, procedures for 
legislative actions, or requests for major or minor development review, conditional use 
permit, or low impact permit.”  
10. This application is being processed as a Conditional Use Permit, per a decision from the 
Community Development Director.  
11. The average pole height of the existing transmission line is ~83 feet.  
12. The average pole height of the upgraded transmission line is ~74 feet.  
13. Heights of poles are determined through this Conditional Use Permit.  
14. The proposed improvements within the unincorporated Summit County take place 
within the Lower Silver Creek Soils Temporary Overlay Zone.  
15. Per Summit County Code Title 4 Health and Sanitation; “Anyone desiring to develop or 
redevelop in the overlay zone shall obtain a soils study and shall show evidence that the 
development area is outside of the impacted area or shall propose a plan to remediate 
any environmental problems/violations identified in the study to the satisfaction of 
UDEQ and EPA before Summit County will grant a development permit.” 
16. The applicant has provided a soils study and has received Utah Department of 
Environmental Quality (UDEQ)/Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) approval for the 
proposed pole replacement taking place within the Lower Silver Creek Soils Temporary 
Overlay Zone.  
17. Due to the challenging geography of the particular area, Rocky Mountain Power is 
requesting to replace the six wooden poles within the unincorporated Summit County 
jurisdiction with self-weathering COR-TEN poles.  
18. Staff has reviewed the proposal for compliance with applicable Development Code 
standards. 
19. Service providers have reviewed the proposal for compliance with applicable standards. 
 
Conclusions of Law: 
 
1. The proposed project, as conditioned, meets the requisite standards set forth in the 
Snyderville Basin Development Code, Section 10-3-5: Conditional Use Permit. 
 
Conditions of Approval: 
 
1. The locations, heights, and design of the proposed facility and associated improvements 
shall be as per the drawings found in Exhibits A and B of this Staff Report. 
2. No outdoor storage is permitted. 
3. No modification to the approved Conditional Use Permit is permitted without the prior 
approval of the Summit County Community Development Department.  
13
9 
Rocky Mountain Power Transmission Line Upgrade CUP Staff Report – September 10, 2024 
4. Final grading shall be reviewed and approved by the Engineering and Planning 
Departments at the grading permit time. 
5. All disturbed area shall be revegetated with a native, drought tolerant seed mix. 
6. A bond shall be in place to insure site restoration/revegetation of the disturbed areas up 
to and including neighboring trees 
7. All necessary permits must be obtained and fees shall be paid prior to the 
commencement of any construction activity, including but not limited to the Summit 
County Engineering and the Summit County Building Departments (when applicable). 
8. All other service provider requirements shall be met. 
 
Public Notice, Meetings and Comments 
  
This item was publicly noticed as a public hearing with possible action by the Snyderville Basin 
Planning Commission.  Notice of the public hearing was published in the newspaper. Courtesy 
postcards were mailed to all property owners within 1,000 feet of the transmission line. 
 
As of the date of this report, no public comment has been received.

Summit County Planning & Zoning

Coalville, UT 84017 
 
 
The accompanying conditional use permit application is to rebuild a segment of a 6.2-mile existing 
Rocky Mountain Power 46kV transmission line, with approximately 0.35 miles (1,853 linear feet) 
within the boundary of Summit County . The transmission line being rebuilt begins at Rocky 
Mountain Power’s Silver Creek Substation and travels along Old Highway 40 and State Route 
248. 
 
Poles one through six will be replaced with corten, weathering steel structures . Existing poles 7 
through 28 will remain in place and a fine mesh material will be installed on the bottom six feet of 
each pole which is also referred to as fire wrapp ing. Any distribution wires will be placed 
underground.  
 
Protecting the communities we serve while providing safe, reliable, and affordable power is one 
of Rocky Mountain Power’s top priorities.  This project will improve Rocky Mountain Power’s 
electrical system, improve safety, and reduce wildfire risk in the a rea.  This is one of several 
projects in area that Rocky Mountain Power is working on and will be implementing over the next 
several years within fire high consequence area s (FHC A).  Along with these efforts, we have 
increased inspections and vegetation management near our facilities to mitigate wildfire risk. 
 
We look forward to working with the county on this and future projects to better serve the 
communities in Summit County.  
 
 
Sincerely,  
 
 
Andy Badger 
Regional Business Manager 
Rocky Mountain Power

EXHIBIT A.11
25
2 
Silver Creek – Kamas 46kV FHCA Rebuild Reclamation Plan 
1. Introduction 
Rocky Mountain Power (RMP) will construct the Silver Creek to Kamas 46kV FHCA Rebuild (Project) in 
compliance with all federal, state, and local regulations and all other applicable permits. The line exists 
within an RMP-internally identified Fire High Consequence Area (FHCA). The existing line currently has a 
“distribution underbuild” (Silver Creek #14), distribution voltage conductor underneath the high-voltage 
transmission conductor – essentially two (2) lines on one pole. RMP’s proposed project can be 
categorized into two (2) parts:  
- Part 1 – Transmission; Silver Creek – Kamas 46kV Rebuild. This entails replacing transmission 
poles from structures 1-6 and 44-87. Approximately 4-miles. Fire wrap should be installed 
on poles to remain in place (Structures 7-43). Additionally, replace the 1/0-ACSR conductor 
between structures 43-77 with 397-ACSR conductor, approximately 1.9 miles. 
- Part 2 – Distribution; Silver Creek #14 Underground. This entails replacing the existing 
distribution under build (Silver Creek #14) per RMP design including undergrounding conduit 
and installing vaults.   
The project will replace, pole-for-pole, existing wooden poles with weathered steel poles, with over-
engineered conductor sizes (138kV) installed to increase clearance spans. While engineered at 138kV 
the line will still be operated at 46kV. The over-engineered transmission insulators will make it necessary 
for the “distribution underbuild” of Silver Creek #14 to be removed and placed underground. These 
designs are to minimize potential of both lines to cause wildfire or be affected by a wildfire in the 
region.   
The project is in Summit and Wasatch Counties of Utah. Originating at the Silver Creek Substation at 
~4136 Atkinson Road Park City, Utah in Summit County and travelling southeast then east to the Kamas 
Substation located in Kamas, Utah Summit County. The line and proposed work crosses into Wasatch 
County in the Deer Mountain Community. The existing line is 30-years or older in age and has been in 
continually servicing Summit and Wasatch County residents and businesses. RMP Operations and 
Maintenance (O&M) have serviced the transmission and distribution line during this time utilizing 
existing access and egress and ingress within the existing right-of-way. The line, and existing access 
exists within Private and Public lands (Jordanelle State Park), the majority within private.  
This Reclamation Plan (Plan) is part of RMP’s efforts to ensure public interest is addressed by presenting 
details regarding the final reclamation phase of the Project. This Plan is intended to be adaptive to 
changing conditions and technologies – a “living document”. RMP will have discretion to update, modify, 
or change this reclamation plan should it be deemed warranted due to site conditions, stakeholders, or 
other factors.  
2. Disturbance Areas 
The entirety of the Project will be constructed in previously disturbed areas within RMP’s existing right-
of-way, and the original routes used to construct the powerline and conduct O&M. Temporary 
disturbance will be required to install the direct-embedded poles, access the right-of-way, establish 
pads, trench, and install underground conduit. Table 1 below shows the quantification of access routes, 
poles, underground conduit, and an estimated amount of work areas, or landing pads, around the base 
of the transmission poles.

2.1 Access 
Right-of-way access would be achieved using existing accessways except for gaining access to complete 
the upgrade of some of the transmission poles. Access exists throughout the project area and has been 
utilized by RMP for O&M activities since the line was installed. To quantify the impact access routes will 
be considered 10’ wide. When utilizing historical access “roll and crush” practices will be done. Travel 
between the existing transmission poles along the right-of-way utilizing “roll-crush” allows for utility 
trucks to access each pole location without the need for access road construction or additional earth 
disturbance and maintains existing natural vegetation by rolling onto and crushing it rather than clearing 
and grubbing. Roll- crush reduces opportunities for invasive species to be implanted into the soil bed 
and reduces potential erosion from the removal of the existing vegetation. No ground disturbance will 
be quantified for portions of right-of-way and access that are driven over while preserving vegetation.   
Established access, including improved dirt roads, paved roads, golf cart paths, etc. will only be used 
when conditions will not lead to damage. This includes during winter when snowplowing will affect the 
roadway and spring when wet conditions will rut. 
In certain areas where access is more difficult it may require improvement of existing corridors. This 
would require the grading and removal of vegetation. In areas where vegetation would be removed 
during grading activities, shrubs would be trimmed back creating woody debris slash piles on the down 
gradient side of the disturbance. Slash piles would be positioned to protect sediment and mitigate 
erosion during the construction process.  
2.2 Installation 
Part 1 – Transmission: The new steel poles are embedded at 10% of their height +2’. For the Project the 
holes will be dug ~10 in depth. To achieve this depth a tracked excavator will be utilized to dig and 
backfill the hole. All spoils will be backfilled and packed to ensure pole stability. The installation of the 
pole and insulation equipment will be done with two (2) Class-VII trucks, one equipped with a personnel 
EXHIBIT A.13
27
4 
Silver Creek – Kamas 46kV FHCA Rebuild Reclamation Plan 
bucket, the other with a claw/crane. To operate these two Class-VII trucks safely some disturbance is 
expected around poles with perceptible slope. Disturbance at these areas is estimated to be 20’x20’ feet 
in diameter or less and are largely dependent on the depth of cut and size of fill slope needed to build 
each “landing” pad as a sage and flat work surface. Poles located at relatively flat areas may require a 
similarly sized temporary work area, but grading activities are not anticipated and vegetation within the 
area would be crushed in place, but root system not disturbed. 
Part 2 – Distribution: The underground conduit will be installed via open-trenching and where feasible 
(constrained by wetlands, development, roadways, etc.) horizontal directional drilling will be utilized. 
The majority of the proposed underground conduit runs adjacent to or underneath existing roadways 
and will lead to minimal disturbances to existing vegetation. It is expected, where open-trenching, a 10’ 
wide pathway of disturbance will occur. Where the trench will only be ~2’ the 10’ figure accounts for the 
width of the excavation equipment.  
2.3 Wreck-Out 
Once weathered steel poles are installed, and conduit transferred, the existing wooden poles will be 
removed. The existing wooden poles will be removed by cutting 6” below grade and taken to a facility 
certified to receive the treated poles for proper disposal. Any soil removed during this operation will be 
used to backfill any depression and return the area immediately surrounding the old pole footprint and 
the new pole as it was. 
3. Resources 
In fulfillment of regulatory requirements RMP will coordinate with the State and County in preparation 
of appropriate permits to analyze the potential impacts of the proposed project and mitigate any 
environmental degradation that could take place. The following are resources evaluated and protected 
by acquired permits or RMP’s own internal operating procedures. 
3.1 Land Use, Recreation, and Transportation 
The existing transmission and distribution exists adjacent to SR-248. Numerous residential development 
and roadways are established. RMP will take precautions during construction activities to notify the 
public of ongoing work and reclaim any damaged roadways to the standard of land use observed prior 
to the Project. 
3.2 Vegetation 
As covered in Section 2.0 it is anticipated that vegetation would be removed only for access to the 
transmission line where not passable via drive & crush, for small areas around each transmission 
structure, and where distribution conduit will be installed via trenching. Areas of land are expected to be 
cleared of vegetation during the Project, RMP will reseed using a site-specific seed mix to establish 
vegetation in all areas disturbed. 
3.3 Wildlife 
The project area is primarily within an sagebrush steppe habitat along the Wasatch Back. This habitat is 
generally conducive to many common wildlife species expected to occur in sagebrush/grassland regions 
of the Wasatch Back. This includes but not limited to Greater Sage Grouse and large ungulate species 
like Moose and Elk. RMP has acquired acknowledgement from the Utah Department of Wildlife 
Resources the proposed project will not affect nearby Greater Sage Grouse communities under the State 
EXHIBIT A.14
28
5 
Silver Creek – Kamas 46kV FHCA Rebuild Reclamation Plan 
of Utah Greater Sage Grouse Conservation Plan (Appendix A). During construction of the project 
attention to wildlife movement will be observed for both RMP’s personnel safety and that of the animal. 
That includes transportation of personnel/equipment and operations. 
3.4 Migratory Birds 
Most bird species likely to occur within the project area are protected by the Migratory Bird Treaty Act 
and the Bald and Golden Eagle Protection Act. Within the Project area no known Bald and Golden Eagle 
nests are documented. Preconstruction avian nest clearance surveys would be completed for ground-
disturbing activities, including vegetation clearing, that would take place during the non-raptor 
migratory bird nesting season. Mortality of avian species is not anticipated to occur due to compliance 
with APLIC guidelines.  
3.5 Water Resources 
Based on review of National Wetlands Inventory data, one riverine feature (Silver Creek), and several 
wetland features are mapped within the portion of the transmission and distribution line ROWs. These 
features have been identified and protected from access. During operations RMP will avoid impacting 
these features with fill or roll-crush practices resulting in the alteration of a potential Water of the US 
(WOUS). Any work necessary near potential wetlands, not delineated and acknowledged by the US Army 
Corps of Engineers (USACE) under Clean Water Act (CWA) Section 404 will be done under the 
Nationwide Permit #33. Where distribution circuit is proposed Horizontal Directional Drilling (HDD) will 
be implemented underneath the potential wetland to preserve the vegetation and hydrology. 
RMP will comply with CWA 402 as necessary and acquire a Construction General Permit as part of the 
Utah Pollutant Discharge Elimination System. A Storm Water Pollution Prevention Plan (SWPPP) will be 
compiled to administer potential sediment and erosion from the Project that has potential to pollute 
storm water and subsequently WOUS. The SWPPP will be kept digitally and onsite to inform the Project 
team of best management practices to reduce impacts to stormwater. 
3.6 Soil Resources 
Part 1 – Transmission line crosses through the Lower Silver Creek Soil Zone (Appendix B). Soil analyses 
have been conducted at the proposed pole replacement locations and an excavation and soil disposal 
plan has been compiled to contain contaminants discovered at within the hazardous soil zone. Existing 
poles will be cut at ground level and the butts abandoned below grade to reduce hazardous material 
from being disturbed. No earthwork is approved inside the Lower Silver Creek Soil Zone except the six 
(6) transmission pole replacements.  
There are multiple soil types in the project area, with varying erosion hazards. Soil exposure to wind and 
water erosional forces will be minimized by waiting as long as feasible prior to beginning construction to 
clear vegetation and disturb soil. To control dust as needed, the applicant may spray ground or road 
surfaces with water. Topsoil, when removed, will be stockpiled separately from other soils to increase 
restoration success. Topsoil will be replaced at appropriate depths when site activities are complete. 
Any ground disturbances created during the removal of the poles and other equipment would be 
backfilled with on-site materials, tamped, and reseeded as a part of final reclamation. 
EXHIBIT A.15
29
6 
Silver Creek – Kamas 46kV FHCA Rebuild Reclamation Plan 
3.7 Cultural Resources  
The Project right-of-way contains no previously identified cultural resources. It is anticipated that all 
eligible cultural resources would be avoided during construction activities resulting in a finding of no 
historic properties affected. Unanticipated discoveries of potential cultural resources will be protected 
by RMP postponing work and contacting Environmental Services to document and determine the 
significance. 
4. Reclamation 
Final reclamation includes recontouring accessways back to the original contour, seeding, controlling 
noxious weeds, and may also include other techniques to improve reclamation success, such as 
constructing waterbars, mulching, and redistributing woody debris.  
Disturbed areas (e.g., staging areas, structure locations, pulling and tensioning stations) will be restored 
to pre-disturbance conditions to the maximum extent practicable following construction activities. 
Disturbed areas would be stabilized (i.e., contours restored, compacted soils loosened, removed 
vegetation scattered etc.) as needed. Accepted and approved erosion protection measures may be used 
to prevent soil loss during the reclamation process. Erosion and sediment control devices would be left 
in place until stabilization has been established.  
Seeding and revegetation is necessary to minimize erosion during inclement weather. The seed mix for 
this project has been developed with the criteria of being drought tolerant and low maintenance. 
Reseeding will be conducted during the first appropriate growing season (Fall or Spring) after 
completion of construction. Seeding may need to be repeated if satisfactory growth is not established 
after the second growing season.  
The seedbed would be prepared prior to seeding, consisting of dragging chains or harrowing (or similar) 
to roughen and loosen the soil surface, specifically to de-compact the soils surface, such as along 
accessways. Seed would then be broadcast and raked in or drilled to ensure seeds have adequate 
contact and cover by soil. Due to safety concerns, seed would be broadcast at areas along the Project 
that are too steep to rake or drill. The following seed mix has been submitted for approved to be used 
along and adjacent to the project corridor. Table seeding rates listed below are for rake and drill 
methods. Multiply seeding rate by 1.5 if broadcast seeding is necessary. Seeding is to be applied during 
the fall seeding window between October 15 and December 15 within the year of completion, if 
possible. Table 2 contains the preferred seeding mix.

Subject:  Rocky Mountain Power, Silver Creek – Kamas 46kV Transmission Line Upgrade 
                
Dear Mr. Jorgensen:     
 
The state of Utah (“State”), through the Public Lands Policy Coordinating Office 
(PLPCO), in coordination with the Utah Division of Wildlife Resources (“DWR”), has  
 reviewed the “Rocky Mountain Power, Silver Creek – Kamas 46kV transmission line upgrade” 
proposal and offers the following greater sage-grouse related comments.  
 
The project is outside of designated Sage Grouse Management Areas and more than three 
miles from the nearest documented sage-grouse breeding location. DWR appreciates Rocky 
Mountain Power’s proactive mitigation approach of limiting construction operations until after 
10 a.m. daily. This effort is consistent with the Utah Conservation Plan for Greater Sage Grouse 
to reduce potential disturbance. The proximity of the project to SR-248 will further minimize any 
possible impacts.  
 
If you have wildlife questions, please contact Josee Seamons, the DWR’s Impact 
Analysis Biologist in our Central Region office, at jseamons@utah.gov or 385-421-1277.

RE: Silver Creek Substation to Kamas 46 KV FHCA Rebuild Project – Richardson Flat 
Tailings Site, Summit County, Utah 
Dear Mr. Jorgensen: 
Rocky Mountain Power (RMP) requested that Hill West collect soil samples near the power poles 
that will be decommissioned and replaced with fire resistant poles along the Silver Creek to Kamas 
corridor as several of the power poles are located within the United States Environmental Protection 
Agency (USEPA) proposed Richardson Flat Tailings National Priorities List (NPL) Superfund site.  
Hill West Environmental, LLC (Hill West) has prepared this report to summarize the results of soil 
sampling completed prior to the replacement of the power poles within the Richardson Flat Tailing 
area (Figures 1 and 2) to characterize soil for proper waste management.   
The Richardson Flat Tailings NPL area has been impacted due to migration of mine tailing from the 
Ontario Mine which is currently owned by the United Park City Mines (UPCM) located upgradient 
from Richardson Flat Tailing area.  The site has been proposed to be placed on the Comprehensive 
Environmental Response, Compensation, and Liability In formation System (CERCLIS) list for 
further investigation/remediation under Superfund.  Figure 3 identifies the impacted zone and power 
pole locations that were sampled.  Power poles outside the impacted zone were not sampled as part 
of this investigation.   
SITE DESCRIPTION 
The following description was obtained from the USEPA website for this area.  The Richardson Flat 
Tailings site covers over 2,700 acres in a small valley 1.5 miles northeast of Park City, Utah.  The 
site consists of a tailing dam and impoundment that were used to capture and hold mill tailing from 
the Ontario Mine near Park City, the tailings that were t ransported downstream to the banks of 
Lower Silver Creek, and an outfall known as Prospector Drain.  Prospector Drain collects shallow 
ground water and discharges the flow partially to a construction treatment wetland and partially to a 
natural wetland area near the banks of Lower Silver Creek.   
In 2007, USEPA and UPCM entered into a consent decree for remedial design and remedial action at 
Operable Unit 1 (OU1).  Work at this location is largely complete.  Under the 2014 AOC, UPCM 
will perform an Engineering Evaluation and Cost Analysis (EE/CA) and non-time critical removal 
EXHIBIT A.22
36
  P a g e | 2 
action for OUs 2 and 3.  OU2 encompasses approximately 1,875 acres along Lower Silver Creek 
north and east of Highway 40.  OU3 encompasses approximately 836 acres located east of Park City 
in areas along Silver Creek.  The power pole replacement project is within OU2 and OU3. 
The primary concerns are the site’s effect on surface water quality in the area, and the potential for 
people or wildlife to come into direct contact with the tailing s or contaminated soil.  Hazardous 
substances at the site include heavy metals such as arsenic, cadmium, copper, lead, mercury, silver, 
and zinc.  Based on USEPA’s letter to Rocky Mountain Power dated July 24, 2020, USEPA has 
established interim residential Site-Specific Screening Levels (SSSLs) for arsenic and lead.  The 
interim screening levels assume conservative land use (i.e., full-time residential) and are considered 
to be protective for all other uses.  The interim screening levels are 19 milligrams per kilogram  
(mg/kg) for arsenic and 262 mg/kg for lead.   
SOIL INVESTIGATION 
On June 5, 2023, Hill West directed E.T. Technologies, Inc. of Salt Lake City, Utah, a licensed Utah 
driller, to advance six (6) direct-push soil borings to 15-feet below ground surface (bgs) within 3 feet 
of the existing power poles (Power Pole Numbers 805 – 810) that service the 46 KV FHCA Rebuild 
Project to determine arsenic and lead concentrations within the soil column.  The arsenic and lead 
concentrations within each liner can then be used to characterize the soil column; and determine if 
hazardous waste disposal is required for each five-foot interval or if the soil can remain in the 
vicinity of the power poles to minimize disposal costs. 
During direct-push drilling activities , soil samples were collected continuously in acrylic liners 
provided by the direct-push drilling contractor.  Each liner was opened, and one (1) composite soil 
sample was collected from each 5-foot liner from ground surface to 5 feet bgs; 6 feet bgs to 10 feet 
bgs; and from 11 feet bgs to 15 feet bgs. 
The soil samples were collected using clean nitrile gloves and placed into laborator y provided 
containers which were then delivered in an iced cooler using chain-of-custody (COC) procedure to 
ChemTech-Ford Laboratories, Inc. ( ChemTech-Ford) in Salt Lake City, Utah, a Utah certified 
analytical laboratory.   Samples were submitted for total arsenic and lead by United States 
Environmental Protection Agency (USEPA) Method 6020A. 
SOIL ASSESSMENT ANALYTICAL RESULTS 
The analytical results for the soil samples collected from the six (6) power pole locations within the 
Richardson Flat Area are summarized below and presented in Tables 1 and Table 2.  Copies of the 
laboratory analytical reports are provided as Attachment B. 
Laboratory analytical results for the soil samples indicated the following: 
• Arsenic was reported above the USEPA site-specific SSSL of 19 mg/kg in the samples 
collected near power pole location 807 (0 to 5 feet bgs), 808 (0 to 5 feet bgs), and 810 (6 to 
10 feet bgs and 11 to 15 feet bgs) as presented in Table 1 below.   
Based on previous toxicity characteristic leaching procedure (TCLP) sampling results, these 
soils have been characterized as a RCRA non-hazardous waste.  However, the reported arsenic 
concentrations reported are above the USEPA site-specific SSSL (19 mg/kg) and will need to

Waste Management 
• Soil in the vicinity of Power Poles 805, 806, and 809 are below the USEPA site-specific 
SSSLs of 19 mg/kg for arsenic and 262 mg/kg for lead.  All soil can remain in the vicinity of 
the power poles or used as backfill for the removed poles. 
• Soil in the vicinity Power Poles 807 and 808 from surface to five (5) feet bgs requires 
containerization due to arsenic and lead concentrations above the USEPA site-specific SSSL 
of 19 mg/kg and 262 mg/kg, respectively.   
• Soil in the vicinity Power Pole 810 from gro und surface to 15 feet bgs requires 
containerization due to arsenic concentrations above the USEPA site -specific SSSL of 
19 mg/kg. 
• All soil above USEPA site-specific SSSLs will need to be containerized into mobile dump 
containers as illustrated in the photograph below: 
 
The soil can then be transported from the excavation site to the designated roll-off containers 
that will be located west of the wash area.  Hill West estimates that up to four (4) roll-off 
containers will be required to containerize all contaminated soil for this project.

CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS 
• Soil within the first 5-feet below ground surface from Power Poles 807 and 808 will need to 
be containerized into lined roll-off containers pending transport and disposal at Clean 
Harbors Grassy Mountain Landfill facility in Grantsville, Utah.   
• All soil (0 – 15 feet bgs) from Power Pole 810 will be containerized into lined roll-off 
containers pending transport and disposal at Clean Harbors Grassy Mountain Landfill facility 
in Grantsville, Utah. 
• All soil in the vicinity of Power Poles 805, 806, and 809 can remain by the power poles and 
reused in the area or used as backfill for the removed poles.

Report Footnotes
Abbreviations
ND = Not detected at the corresponding Minimum Reporting Limit (MRL).
1 mg/L = one milligram per liter or 1 mg/kg = one milligram per kilogram   = 1 part per million.
1 ug/L  = one microgram per liter or 1 ug/kg = one microgram per kilogram = 1 part per billion.
1 ng/L  = one nanogram per liter or 1 ng/kg  = one nanogram per kilogram   = 1 part per trillion.
On calculated parameters, there may be a slight difference between summing the rounded values shown on the report 
vs the unrounded values used in the calculation.

SNYDERVILLE BASIN PLANNING COMMISSION AGENDA ITEM REPORT

STAFF REPORT 
 
To:   Snyderville Basin Planning Commission   
From:   Jennifer Strader, Senior Planner 
Date of Meeting: September 10, 2024 
Type of Item:  Plat Amendment - Public Hearing, Possible Action 
Process:  Administrative Review 
File #:   24-118 
 
 
Recommendation: Staff has reviewed the application for compliance with all standards 
in the Snyderv ille Basin Development Code (Code) and found that it m eets the minimum 
required for approval.  Staff recommends that the Snyderville Basin Planning  Commission 
(SBPC) review the proposed  Plat Amendment , conduct a  public hearing and approve the  
application, pursuant to the findings of fact, conclusions of law, and conditio ns of approval 
found in this Staff Report. 
 
Project Description 
Project Name:   The Preserve, Phase 2, Lot 21 Plat Amendment 
Applicant(s):   Ted Mason  
Property Owner(s):  Paul Kirincich & Kelly Kruse 
Location:   312 West Deer Hill 
Zone District:   Hillside Stewardship (HS) 
Parcel Number and Size: PRESRV-2-21, 12.30 acres 
Final Land Use Authority: Snyderville Basin Planning Commission (SBPC) 
 
Proposal 
The applicant, Ted Mason, on behalf of the property owners, Paul Kirincich and Kelly Kruse, is 
requesting to amend the location of the Development Activity Envelope on Lot 21, The 
Preserve, Phase 2 Subdivision. The existing Development Activity Envelope contains 30,000 SF; 
the amended envelope will also contain 30,000 SF (Exhibit A). 
 
The requested amendment would allow construction in a manner that utilizes the existing 
topography and allows the primary residence and guest house to be located closer together 
which minimizes the visual impact from the road. The amended Development Activity Envelope 
would also allow better turnaround capabilities for fire truck and emergency vehicle access. 
 
68
2 
 
Vicinity Map 
 
 
Background 
The Preserve, Phase 2 Subdivision was recorded on 12.7.2024. It consists of 21 lots, each 
containing a Development Activity Envelope.  
 
The Preserve, Phase 2 Subdivision is governed by the Second Amendment to the Red Hawk 
Wildlife Preserve Project Consent Agreement (Agreement). Section IV (A) of the Agreement 
states:  
 
 “Prior to obtaining a building permit for a lot within the Project, the Owner and the 
 Foundation’s Design Review and Land Use Committee shall mutually agree on the 
 location of a Development Activity Envelope for the lot. The Development Activity 
 Envelope shall not exceed 50,000 square feet, and its location shall not adversely impact 
 any Critical Wildlife and Open Space Areas the otherwise be acceptable to the 
 Foundation’s wildlife consultants and the Director…An owner within the Project may not 
 relocate the Development Activity Envelope without the written approval of Developer 
 and the Foundation’s wildlife consultants.”  
 
Staff received a letter, dated January 17, 2024, from The Preserve’s Design Review Committee 
granting approval of the amendment to the Development Activity Envelope (Exhibit B). 
 
69
3 
 
Analysis and Findings 
Section 10-3-18 (I) of the Snyderville Basin Development Code sets the following grounds for 
vacating or changing a plat: 
 
1. If the county manager or land use authority is satisfied that neither the public nor any 
 person will be materially injured by the proposed vacation, alteration or amendment, 
 the county manager or land use authority may vacate, alter or amend the plat, any 
 portion of the plat, or any road or lot. 
  
 Analysis: The requested amendment would allow construction in a manner that utilizes 
 the existing topography and allows the primary residence and guest house to be located 
 closer together which minimizes the visual impact from the road. The amended 
 Development Activity Envelope would also allow better turnaround capabilities for fire 
 truck and emergency vehicle access. Staff finds no evidence that the public nor any 
 person would be materially injured by the amendment. COMPLIES 
 
2. No plat amendment shall be approved which results in an increase in density. 
 
 Analysis: Density is defined in the Code as, “The number of dwelling units per acre with 
 respect to residential land uses.” The applicant is requesting to amend the location of a 
 Development Activity Envelope on an existing residential lot. The amendment would not 
 result in an additional residential unit. 
 
Recommendation 
Staff recommends that the Snyderville Basin Planning Commission (SBPC) review the proposed 
Plat Amendment , conduct a  public hearing, and approve the  application, pursuant t o the 
following findings of fact, conclusions of law, and conditions of approval. 
 
Findings of Fact: 
1. On July 22, 2024, the applicant, Ted Mason, on behalf of the property owners, Paul 
 Kirincich & Kelly Kruse, submitted a plat amendment application to amend the 
 Development Activity Envelope on Lot 21, The Preserve, Phase 2 Subdivision (PRESRV-2-
 21).  
2. Parcel PRESRV-2-21 is located at 312 West Deer Hill. 
3. Parcel PRESRV-2-21 is located in the Hillside Stewardship Zone. 
4. Parcel PRESRV-2-21 contains 12.30 acres. 
5. The Preserve, Phase 2 Subdivision was recorded on 12.7.2024. It consists of 21 lots, each 
 containing a Development Activity Envelope.  
6. The Preserve, Phase 2 Subdivision is governed by the Second Amendment to the Red 
 Hawk Wildlife Preserve Project Consent Agreement (Agreement).  
7. Section IV (A) of the Agreement states:  
 
70
4 
 
 “Prior to obtaining a building permit for a lot within the Project, the Owner and the 
 Foundation’s Design Review and Land Use Committee shall mutually agree on the 
 location of a Development Activity Envelope for the lot. The Development Activity 
 Envelope shall not exceed 50,000 square feet, and its location shall not adversely impact

 relocate the Development Activity Envelope without the written approval of Developer 
 and the Foundation’s wildlife consultants.”  
8. Staff received a letter, dated January 17, 2024, from The Preserve’s Design Review 
 Committee granting approval of the amendment to the Development Activity Envelope. 
9. The requested amendment would allow construction in a manner that utilizes the 
 existing topography and allows the primary residence and guest house to be located

STAFF REPORT 
 
To:   Snyderville Basin Planning Commission   
From:   Ray Milliner, County Planner  
Date of Meeting: September 10, 2024 
Type of Item:  Conditional Use Permit - Public Hearing, Possible Action 
Process:  Administrative Review 
File #:   24-056 
 
 
Recommendation: Staff has reviewed the application for compliance with all standards 
in the Snyderv ille Basin Development Code (Code) and found that it m eets the minimum 
required for approval.  Staff recommends that the Snyderville Basin Planning  Commission 
(SBPC) review the proposed Conditional Use Permit, conduct a public hearing and approve the  
application, pursuant to the findings of fact, conclusions of law, and conditio ns of approval 
found in this Staff Report. 
 
Project Description 
 
Project Name:   Maverik Convenience Store 
Applicant(s):   Maverik Inc. (James Copeland Representative) 
Property Owner(s):  Big Hoss Properties LLC. 
Location:   Old Hwy 40 and Atkinson Road 
Zone District: Service Commercial (SC), Hillside Stewardship (HS) Lower Silver 
Creek Soils Overlay zones 
Parcel Number and Size: Parcel #SS-65-A-3, 7.68 acres 
Type of Process:  Conditional Use Permit 
Final Land Use Authority: Planning Commission  
 
Proposal 
 
Maverick Inc. would like to build a gas station with an attached convenience store at the corner 
of Old HWY 40 and Atkinson Road (Quinn’s Junction). The proposed fuel station is to have a 
5,951 square foot convenience store with 16 vehicle fueling positions, an additional 16 future 
fueling positions and 6 commercial truck / RV fueling positions, for a total of 38 fueling 
positions. 
 
 
77
2 
 
 
Vicinity Map 
 
 
 
 
Background
 
 
Maverick Inc. Has applied for a conditional use permit for a gasoline service station with 
convenience store at the corner of Old HWY 40 and Atkinson Road. Details of the project 
include: 
 
• The lot is approximately 7.68 acres. 
• There is a 2.96-acre wetland area on the northeast side of the lot. No construction is 
proposed in the wetland area.  
• The lot is in both the Service Commercial (SC) and Hillside Stewardship (HS) zones.  
• The lot is in the Lower Silver Creek Soils Overlay Zone.  
78
3 
 
• Proposed construction will take place within the area zoned SC. No construction 
proposed in the HS zone. 
• The proposed fuel station is to have a 5,951 square foot convenience store with 16 
vehicle fueling positions, an additional 16 future fueling positions and 6 commercial 
truck / RV fueling positions, for a total of 38 fueling positions. 
• There is a Fueling canopy proposed above all pumps. 
• Underground fuel tanks are proposed on the west side of property in front of the main 
pump area.  
• 30 parking stalls proposed along the southern side of the building (this number has been 
reduced from 37 stalls). 
• One ingress/egress access point proposed from Old HWY 40. 
• One ingress only access point proposed from Atkinson Road.  
• The applicant has secured water rights for the use which will be provided by Summit 
Water.  
• Sewer service for the use will be provided by the Snyderville Basin Water Reclamation 
District.  
 
In May of 2024, the County Council, with a positive recommendation from the Snyderville Basin 
Planning Commission, approved an amendment to the Snyderville Basin Development Code to 
make a “Gas Station With an Attached Convenience Store” a conditional use in the SC zone. 
This amendment enabled the applicant to apply for this CUP. 
 
August 13, 2024 Planning Commission Meeting 
 
This item was heard by the Planning Commission on August 13, 2024. The Commission 
requested more information on: 
 
• Accessibility for bikers and hikers from the Rail Tral and requested that the applicant 
address access to the site. The applicant enhanced the rail trail access by providing a 
pathway around the perimeter of the of the site from the new Old Highway 40 road.  
• Onsite parking. The Commission requested that the applicant reduce the amount of 
parking. The applicant reduced the number of parking spaces by 7.  
• The new “Old Highway 40” plans. The commission requested more detail on the 
proposed Old Highway 40 plans.  
 
Each of these items was addressed by the applicant and are included in the attached exhibits.  
 
Analysis and Findings

The subject property is in the SC zone district.

The purpose of the service commercial/light 
industrial (SC) zone district is to provide 
appropriate locations within the Snyderville Basin 
for service commercial and light industrial uses, 
recognizing that such uses provide vital support 
functions for the basin's resort and residential 
economy.

to the Development Code making

Chapter 8, General Provisions  
The proposed gas station is subject to the following requirements 
 
A. Separation: No service station shall be located within two hundred feet (200') of a single-
family dwelling or within five hundred feet (500') of another service station. COMPLIES 
 
Analysis: There are no single-family homes within 200 feet of the lot and no other 
gas stations within 500 feet of the lot.  
 
B. Distance to Intersection, Road: No more than one service station may be located within 
two hundred feet (200’) of the intersection of any major road, arterial road or collector 
road with another major road, arterial road or collector road. COMPLIES 
 
Analysis: The lot is approximately 400 feet from the intersection of HWY 248 and 
HWY 40. Nonetheless, there are no other service stations in the immediate area.  
 
C. Screening: An opaque screening treatment shall be provided on all sides of the property 
that are located adjacent to residentially zoned and residentially used property. COMPLIES 
 
Analysis: There are no residential developments on any side of the property. The 
north, east and west sides are industrial, and the south side is a toxic waste 
dump. Staff finds that there is no need for opaque screening.

Analysis: The use does have the potential for environmental issues, mostly related to

84

as well as Utah State requirements for underground tanks. The potential impacts of the

property owner has signed an agreement with the EPA and DEQ finalizing the process.  
 
There are no service stations on HWY 248 between Kamas and Park City. This means 
that any resident living along that corridor (including Brown’s Canyon) must drive to 
Kamas or Park City to get gas. Allowing a service station use in the Quinn’s Junction area 
could reduce vehicle trips into Park City, Kamas or Silver Summit by providing an 
alternative for those residents.

Transportation Departments to make improvements and relocate Old HWY 40 that will 
help both the applicant and drivers on 228 and the frontage road.

character and purpose provision of the applicable zoning district and will not adversely affect 
surrounding land uses.

uses allowed in the immediate area. There are two major arterial highways intersecting 
nearby with loads of traffic. Conditions of approval have been included in this report 
designed to mitigate the impacts of the use on traffic in the area.

Recommendation 
Staff recommends that the Snyderville Basin Planning Commission (SBPC) review the proposed 
Conditional Use Permit, conduct a public hearing, and approve the application, pursuant to the 
following findings of fact, conclusions of law, and conditions of approval. 
 
Findings of Fact: 
 
1. Parcel #SS-65-A-3 is located at the intersection of Atkinson Road and Old HWY 40.  
2. The lot is approximately 7.68 acres. 
3. There is a 2.96-acre wetland area on the northeast side of the lot. No construction is 
proposed in the wetland area. 
4. There are slopes exceeding 30% between the wetland area and the proposed 
development area. No construction is proposed on the steep slope area.   
5. The lot is in both the Service Commercial (SC) and Hillside Stewardship (HS) zones.  
6. The lot is in the Lower Silver Creek Soils Overlay Zone.  
7. In May of 2024, the County Council approved an amendment to the Development Code 
making Gas Stations with attached Convenience stores a conditional use in the SC zone.
 
8. Proposed construction will take place within the area zoned SC. No construction 
proposed in the HS zone. 
9. The proposed fuel station is to have a 5,951 square foot convenience store with 16 
vehicle fueling positions, an additional 16 future fueling positions and 6 commercial 
truck / RV fueling positions, for a total of 38 fueling positions. 
10. Fueling canopy proposed above all pumps. 
11. Underground fuel tanks proposed on west side of property in front of main pump area.  
12. 30 parking stalls proposed along the southern side of the building. 
13. One ingress/egress access point proposed from Old HWY 40. 
14. One ingress only access point proposed from Atkinson Road.  
86
11 
 
15. In January of 2021, the applicant signed an agreement with the EPA and DEQ outlining 
the process that would allow them to develop the lot.  
16. The agreement requires the property owner to conduct a survey of the property with 
specific requirements that would need to be followed to test the lot and to receive 
approval to begin development.  
17. In January of 2024, the applicant conducted an environmental impact study of the lot.  
18. The study followed the requirements of both the County and the EPA.  
19. The study found that the northwest side of the lot is acceptable for commercial uses, 
but that the wetland area is managed through an environmental covenant and is 
therefore undevelopable.  
20. Because this is a new development and it is not under a development agreement or 
consent agreement, the project must meet the requirements of Chapter 5, Affordable 
Housing in the Snyderville Basin Development code. 
21. Based on the formula in Chapter 5, the applicant is responsible for .35 AUEs (affordable 
Unit Equivalents).  
22. The applicant has chosen to pay an in-lieu fee of $30,313.50 instead of building an 
affordable unit.  
23. The maximum allowed building height in the SC zone is 32 feet above existing grade or 
finished grade, whichever results in the smaller building.  
24. The proposed structure will be 29 feet above finished grade.  
25. The SC zone requires a 80 foot front yard setback, and 12 foot side and rear yard 
setbacks.  
26. The proposed setbacks are 80 feet in the front, 67 feet and 38 feet on the sides and 57 
feet in the rear.  
27. The proposed use has 30 parking spaces. 
28. All necessary utilities are available on site.  
29. The applicant has secured water rights for the use which will be provided by Summit 
Water.  
30. Sewer service for the use will be provided by the Snyderville Basin Water Reclamation 
District.  
31. All proposed loading and unloading will take place on site.  
32. The perimeter landscape area is 18,900 square feet or approximately 28% of the parking 
area.  
33. There are no service stations on HWY 248 between Kamas and Park City.  
34. This area of Summit County is industrial.  
35. There are no residential uses allowed in the immediate area.  
36. There are two major arterial highways intersecting nearby. 
37. All proposed loading and unloading of merchandise, including fuel will occur on site.

Conclusions of Law:  
 
1. The use is in accordance with the general plan. 
2. The use conforms to all applicable provisions of the Code, including, but not limited to, 
any applicable provisions of Section 10-3-5, Chapter 4, and state and federal regulations. 
3. The use is not detrimental to public health, safety, and welfare. 
4. The use is appropriately located with respect to public facilities. 
5. The use is compatible with the existing neighborhood character and with the purpose 
provisions of the applicable zone district and will not affect surrounding land uses. 
 
Conditions of Approval: 
 
1. Prior to the issue of a certificate of occupancy, the applicant shall install a southbound 
left turn pocket at the Maverik access along Old Hwy 40 
2. Prior to the issue of a certificate of occupancy the applicant shall install a northbound 
deceleration lane at the Maverik access along Old Hwy 40 
3. Maverik will be required to work with UDOT and install a traffic signal at the SR-248/Old 
Hwy 40 intersection. 
4. A certificate of occupancy for the use shall not be issued until the County Engineer and 
Transportation Director are satisfied that the road improvements have been made.  
5. The applicant shall pay an in-lieu fee of $30,313.50 to meet the affordable housing 
requirement of the Development Code.  
6. All mechanical equipment for the convenience store shall be screened from view from 
public roads. 
7. No signs are approved as part of this Conditional Use Permit. The applicant shall submit 
a sign application for review and approval prior to the installation of any signs.

Executive Summary 
 
I. Introduction and Summary 
 
A. Purpose of Report and Study Objectives 
 
The proposed development site, a 7.65 parcel, is located on the north side of SR-248 to the east of 
Frontage Road and south of Atkinson Road in Summit County, Utah. 
 
Summit County is currently in the process of completing final designs for the intersection of the Frontage 
Road and SR-248 to be relocated approximately 225 feet to the east of its current l ocation.   
 
This traffic study will look at the impacts of the proposed Maverik development.  
 
Figure One shows the location of the development. Trip projection and traffic analysis in this report will 
be performed for the AM and PM peak hours. 
 
B. Executive Summary 
 
For the SR-248 and Frontage Road intersection, the Synchro analysis shows that it will not operate at 
acceptable levels of service. The is because the southbound left turn will drop to LOS F during the peak 
hours with the site.  The signal warrant study found that the Peak Hour, Eight-Hour and Four -Hour signal 
warrants were met and that a traffic signal would operate efficiently.  
 
For the Maverik Access and the Frontage Road, the Synchro analysis shows that it will operate 
adequately with the proposed development. The Synchro analysis showed that the provided storage 
lengths are more than adequate for the projected queues. The left turn and right turn auxiliary lane checks 
showed that a northbound right turn lane would be required based on typical UDOT thresholds for a decel 
lane.  Similarly, a southbound left turn pocket would also be requ ired based on the typical UDOT 
thresholds. 
 
For Atkinson Road and the Frontage Road, the Synchro analysis shows that it will operate adequately 
with the proposed development. The Synchro analysis showed that the provided storage lengths are more 
than adequate for the projected queues. The left turn and right turn auxiliary lane checks showed that a 
northbound right turn lane would meet the typical UDOT thresholds for a decel lane.  The so uthbound 
traffic was not enough to meet the threshold, so a left turn pocket was not required. 
 
There is potential need for Maverik to contribute to the Frontage Road design elements or a potential 
traffic signal at SR-248 and the Frontage Road.  The study showed that new Maverik traffic contributed 
54 percent of the total traffic at the Maverik Access  with the Frontage Road.  For the SR-248 and 
Frontage Road signal, 12 percent of the traffic was related to new Maverik traffic.  
 
The developer is recommended to install off-site signing, striping and pavement markings in accordance 
with current Summit County specifications and the most current edition of the Manual on Uniform Traffic 
Control Devices (MUTCD).

II.  Proposed Development 
 
The proposed fuel station is to have a 5,951 square foot convenience store with 16 vehicle fueling 
positions, an additional 16 future fueling positions and 6 commercial truck / RV fueling positions, for a 
total of 38 fueling positions. 
 
Two accesses are planned for this site, Access A and Access B. Access A is proposed as a full movement 
access on the Frontage Road.  Access B is proposed as an entrance only, mainly for truck/vehicle 
separation.  Access B will be located on Atkinson Roa d.  
 
  
III.  Area Conditions 
 
Trip projection and traffic analysis in this report will be performed for the AM and PM peak hours.   
 
The intersection configuration at SR-248 and the Frontage Road is as follows:  
 
• SR-248, eastbound/westbound, has a dedicated left turn lane, and two through lanes.  The 
westbound traffic has a right turn deceleration lane. 
• Frontage Road, northbound/southbound has one lane in each direction with no center turn lane.  
At the intersection of SR-248, a southbound left turn pocket is avai lable. 
 
The speed limit on SR-248, is 55 mph. The speed limit on the Frontage Road is 40 mph.  
 
Figure Two shows the conceptual site plan.

A. Existing Traffic Data 
 
Intersection counts were made on Thursday, November 2, 2023, from 7:00 - 9:00 a.m. and 4:00 - 6:00 
p.m. The counts were made at the following intersections: 
 
• SR-248 and Frontage Road 
• Frontage Road and Atkinson Road 
 
The AM Peak Hour was from 7:45 – 8:45 a.m. The PM Peak Hour was from 4:15 - 5:15 p.m.   
 
Detailed counts are shown in the Appendix.  
 
Figure Three shows the existing traffic counts for the project site.    
 
B. 5-year traffic growth Projection 
 
The study parameters included traffic projections over the next five years, an annual growth rate of 
2% was used for a growth factor of 1.10.  This growth rate was based on reviewing the projected 
growth along SR-248.  Utah’s Unified Transportation Plan shows that on SR-248 to the east of the 
Frontage Road was 12,119 ADT in 2019 growing to 22,000 in 2050. This equated to a 1.95% growth 
rate or growth factor of 1.82 over 31 years. Therefore, the conservatively rounded 2% growth rate 
was used for future five-year projections. 
 
Figure Four shows the 2028 background traffic projected counts.

IV. Projected Traffic 
 
While the Institute of Transportation Engineers (ITE) Trip Generation (Eleventh Edition) handbook is 
typically used to estimate trips for land uses, AWA has transaction data for Maverik stores that are more 
accurate than ITE. In recent studies in Douglas, Wyoming and North Las Vegas, Nevada, it was found 
that the transaction data yielded trip rates that were conservative as compared to actual trip counts.   
 
The proposed land use utilized for this analysis is as follows: 
 
• Maverik Convenience Store – 36 fueling positions. 
 
The total transactions included fuel purchases (passenger and heavy vehicles) and in-store transactions. 
The total transactions were multiplied by 2.0 to estimate trips to/from the site. Total transactions times 2.0 
should be a conservative estimation of trips generated as it is more common for additional transactions 
(i.e., a fueling vehicle making separate transactions for fuel and food) than it is for less transactions (i.e., 
walk in purchases, no purchases after driving on the site, etc). 
 
Table One shows the locations that were sampled (eleven stores), fueling positions and trips generated 
based on total transactions times 2.0.  
 
The data shows what is expected, Maverik stores have a greater peak in the AM due to workers 
purchasing food. The transaction data also has a mix of heavy vehicles, which do not fuel as quickly as 
passenger car vehicles.  These blended fueling positions yield a lower trip ra te than passenger car vehicles 
only. 
 
Primary Trips  
 
Primary trips represent those trips which are not already passing by the site. These trips could be 
direct trips to the site from another location or diverted trips from other streets in the area. 
 
Pass-by Trips 
 
Pass-by trips were estimated.  Pass-by trips represent those trips which already pass the site as 
background traffic. This traffic already exists on the roadway and does not constitute additional 
traffic.  
 
ITE suggests that fuel centers such as this will have pass-by rates over 50 percent. In considering the 
major streets that this development will front, it is considered reasonable to assume a 50 percent pass-
by rate.

Pass-by Traffic 
 
For pass-by trips, it should be noted that the traffic already exists on the highway and is diverted to the 
new site. The diversions from the highway are repres ented with negative trips. The Maverik transactions 
show that approximately 10 percent of overall transactions are truck fueling , so 10 percent of pass-by 
traffic was projected to enter at Atkinson Road access.  The pass-by assumptions were based on the 
average of AM/PM traffic and are as follows:  
 
• 40% - Westbound right-in/right-out from SR-248 
• 35% - Eastbound left-in/left-out from SR-248 
• 25% - Frontage Road  
o 15% Northbound right-in/right-out 
o 10% Southbound left-in/left-out

transactions are truck fueling, so 10 percent of primary traffic was projected to enter at Atkinson Road 
(Access B):

V. Traffic Analysis 
 
The accesses and intersections were analyzed based on the Synchro software to evaluate the impacts of 
the project on the surrounding traffic network. As was discussed pre viously, the alternatives compare 
existing traffic and existing traffic with the proposed Maverik fuel station (with site).

B. Signal Warrant Analysis 
 
Warrant 3 – Peak Hour Volumes  
 
The volumes will be listed for both “AM/(PM)”. The traffic on SR-248 has a total eastbound volume of 
793/(803) and a westbound volume of 826/(887) at SR-248 and the Frontage Road.  This total two-way 
volume of 1,619/(1,690) vehicles per hour (Major Street) is one of the factors for determining whether a 
signal would be warranted for Warrant 3, Peak Hour Volume.   
 
As this volume far exceeds the threshold in for the Major Street of the Peak Hour Warrant, the Minor 
Street Volume must only exceed 100 vehicles per hour for a traffic signal to be warranted (see Figure 4C-
4 for Peak Hour Warrant on a Major Street above 40 mph).  The following page shows a plot of the Peak 
Hour Warrant based on MUTCD Figure 4C-4.  The projected southbound left turn traffic at the proposed 
signal would exceed Warrant 3 requirements, Peak Hour Volume. These volumes projected for the 
southbound left turns is: 170/(139) for the fuel center traffic. Therefore, the peak hour warrant will be 
met. 
 
Based on the above projections, Warrant 3 would be met.  It should be noted that it is currently 
near meeting Peak Hour Warrants without the Maverik projected traffic. 
 
 
AM - Major Street – 1,619 
Minor Street - 170 
PM - Major Street – 1,690 
Minor Street - 139

To provide additional signal warrant information for Warrant 2 (Four Hour Vehicular Volume), Warrant 
1 (Eight Hour Vehicular) and Warrant 7 (Crash Experience), counts and crash data were obtained from 
UDOT (US-40/SR-248 east side, signal performance metrics from 11/22/23) and from Summit County 
for the Frontage Road (12/30/2020).  This data was developed to show how other hours of the day 
compare to the peak hour of fuel center traffic. Only the percentage of peak hour from these counts, 
applied to the volumes in the traffic study, will be used for signal warrant analysis. These counts show 
that traffic is fairly consistent throughout the day.

Warrant 1 – Eight-Hour Vehicular Volume 
 
For the Eight-Hour Vehicular Volume warrant, Condition B – Interruption of Continuous Traffic, 630 
vehicles per hour on SR-248 (total of both directions) is required for the eight-hour period. This part of 
the warrant is projected at 1,487 vehicles (1,690 x 0.88), so well over the threshold.  
 
To meet the eight-hour warrant Condition B, the proposed signal would have to maintain at least 70 
southbound left turns per hour during the lowest volume of the highest 4 hours. This would yield 76 (90 x 
0.84) for the 2028 background without the site. The site is projected to add another 81 (96 x 0.84). This 
would yield a projected total of 157 minor street left turns for the eight-hour warrant. Therefore, both 
thresholds are met. 
 
Based on the above projections, Warrant 1 would be met without the Maverik, and exceeded with 
the proposed Maverik. 
141
 
23 
 
Warrant 2 – Four-Hour Vehicular Volume 
 
The next figure shows the MUTCD Figure 4C-2 for the four-hour vehicle warrant. While counts were not 
made for all hours, the major street volume was found to be 92 percent of the peak hour volume, based on 
UDOT counts for the same day at US-40/SR-248 east side. For the Frontage Road, the minor street 
volume was found to be 90 percent of the Frontage Road counts provided by Summit County.  
 
To meet the four-hour warrant, the proposed signal would have to maintain at least 80 southbound left 
turns per hour during the lowest volume of the highest 4 hours. This would yield 81 (90 x 0.90) for the 
2028 background without the site. The site is projected to add another 79 (88 x 0.90). This would yield a 
projected total of 159 minor street left turns for the four-hour warrant. Therefore, both thresholds are met 
as can be seen in the nomograph below. 
 
Warrant 2 – Four-Hour Vehicular Volume 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
(1) The four-hour major street volume was estimated as a minimum of 92 percent of the peak hour volume (1,690), 
based on UDOT counts for the same day. 
(2) The minor street volume was estimated based on 90 percent of both the 2028 background traffic plus 90 percent of 
the Maverik generated traffic.  
 
Based on the above projections, Warrant 2 would be met without the Maverik, and exceeded with 
the proposed Maverik. 
 
Major Street – 1,554(1) 
Minor Street – 159(2) 
142
 
24 
 
Warrant 7 – Crash Experience 
 
The crash data showed that six angle related crashes or nearly 66 percent of the crashes at this intersection 
were angle related from 2019 - 2022. Five crashes that could be resolved by a signal, in a year, is the 
threshold to meet Warrant 7 - Crash Experience warrant in MUTCD. In 2021, there were three angle 
related crashes. These crashes could increase with the p roposed development. 
 
Based on the accident data, Warrant 7 would not be met. 
 
Based on the signal warrant discussion and projections in this addendum, the following signal warrants 
were shown to be met: 
 
Warrant 3 – Peak Hour Volumes 
Warrant 2 – Four-Hour Vehicular Volume 
Warrant 1 – Eight-Hour Vehicular Volume 
 
C. Frontage Road Design Data 
 
The traffic impact study used the UDOT threshold of 10 left turns per hour to determine if a southbound 
left turn pocket was needed along the Frontage Road. Based on this threshold (51 projected southbound 
left turns), a southbound left turn pocket is needed at the Maverik access.  With th e need for a southbound 
left turn pocket at SR-248, then it is reasonable to extend the center left turn lane from the Maverik access 
to SR-248.  Based on the same threshold, a southbound left turn pocket at Atkinson Road was not needed 
(7 projected southbound left turns). 
 
For northbound right turns into the Maverik access, the UDOT threshold of 25 right turns was used to 
determine if there was a need for a deceleration lane.  The study showed 185 right turns, so a deceleration 
lane would be needed. Based on the same threshold a deceleration lane would also be needed at A tkinson 
Road due to the exiting traffic (total projected traffic is 66 of which 47 exist and 19 are projected from the 
Maverik). 
 
D. Maverik Traffic Percentages 
 
To project possible contributions to the design features of the re located Frontage Road and a potential 
signal at the Frontage Road and SR-248, the percent of Maverik traffic was calculated.  
 
For the Maverik Access to the Frontage Road, it was calculated that 54 percent of the projected traffic 
will be new Maverik traffic related. 
 
For the SR-248 and Frontage Road intersection, it was calculated that 12 percent of the traffic was 
projected to be new Maverik traffic. 
 
 
143
 
25 
 
 
VI. Conclusions/Recommendations 
 
For the SR-248 and Frontage Road intersection, the Synchro analysis shows that it will not operate at 
acceptable levels of service. The is because the southbound left turn will drop to LOS F during the peak 
hours with the site.  The signal warrant study found that the Peak Hour, Eight-Hour and Four -Hour signal 
warrants were met and that a traffic signal would operate efficiently.  
 
For the Maverik Access and the Frontage Road, the Synchro analysis shows that it will operate 
adequately with the proposed development. The Synchro analysis showed that the provided storage 
lengths are more than adequate for the projected queues. The left turn and right turn auxiliary lane checks 
showed that a northbound right turn lane would be required based on typical UDOT thresholds for a decel 
lane.  Similarly, a southbound left turn pocket would also be requ ired based on the typical UDOT 
thresholds. 
 
For Atkinson Road and the Frontage Road, the Synchro analysis shows that it will operate adequately 
with the proposed development. The Synchro analysis showed that the provided storage lengths are more 
than adequate for the projected queues. The left turn and right turn auxiliary lane checks showed that a 
northbound right turn lane would meet the typical UDOT thresholds for a decel lane.  The so uthbound 
traffic was not enough to meet the threshold, so a left turn pocket was not required. 
 
There is potential need for Maverik to contribute to the Frontage Road design elements or a potential 
traffic signal at SR-248 and the Frontage Road.  The study showed that new Maverik traffic contributed 
54 percent of the total traffic at the Maverik Access  with the Frontage Road.  For the SR-248 and 
Frontage Road signal, 12 percent of the traffic was related to new Maverik traffic.  
 
The developer is recommended to install off-site signing, striping and pavement markings in accordance 
with current Summit County specifications and the most current edition of the Manual on Uniform Traffic 
Control Devices (MUTCD).
